The elliptic three-loop integrals of hadronic vacuum polarization in chiral perturbation theory
Laurent Lellouch, Alessandro Lupo, Mattias Sjö, and Pierre Vanhove

Abstract
This work presents a detailed account of the Feynman integrals required for the three-loop hadronic vacuum polarization calculation performed in 33 . We explain how to compute each of the three-loop integrals, and outline the mathematical framework underlying their evaluation. This culminates in a practical numerical implementation that enables fast and accurate evaluation of these integrals for arbitrary complex values of the photon virtuality.



1
Introduction

In the perturbative calculation of any particle physics observable, nontrivial loop integrals will eventually appear at a sufficiently high order in the perturbative series.
Here, that order is three-loop or N
3
LO in chiral perturbation theory
21
;
22
, and the observable is the hadronic vacuum polarization, a key contribution to the very low-energy QCD corrections to the magnetic moments of leptons.
We performed this calculation in a recent paper
33
, to which we defer the reader for a more complete picture of the physical background.
However, several novel loop integrals that arise there are sufficiently complicated that we have chosen to dedicate a paper to them.

As explained in ref.
33
, vacuum polarization receives contributions from six elliptic master integrals denoted
E_{i}(d;t)
, evaluated in their finite two-dimensional form
E_{i}(2;t)
, or in the finite part
\bar{E}_{i}(4;t)
of their four-dimensional form.
These master integrals belong to the broader class of elliptic integrals, i.e., integrals that cannot be expressed in terms of logarithms, polylogarithms, or multiple polylogarithms (i.e., iterated integrals of logarithms and rational functions).
Instead, they require elliptic functions.

For any given loop integral, the ideal case is to have a closed-form solution, but for integrals as complicated as these, one normally has to resort to numerical methods.
Although a complete overview of the state of the art is beyond the scope of this work, we benchmark our solutions against three existing codes:

pySecDec

9
;
10
;
25
;
26
, a mature general-purpose loop integrator based on sector decomposition;

AMFlow

34
, a more novel approach leveraging integration-by-parts relations to obtain much higher precision than
pySecDec
at the cost of much slower calculations; and

FeynTrop

8
;
7
, another novel approach based on tropical decomposition, which is capable of very quickly obtaining a few digits of precision for finite integrals.
There also exist methods based on numerical solutions to the differential equations satisfied by Feynman integrals
27
;
1
;
34
,
which require initial conditions obtained via other numerical or analytical methods, although an alternative approach that bypasses this requirement has been proposed
14
.
Lastly, some elliptic loop integrals are known in terms of elliptic polylogarithms, for which there exist implementations
44
;
15
based on
GiNaC

4
.
However, these implementations lack analytic continuation beyond the radius of convergence of the defining power series, although this has been addressed through a representation in terms of iterated integrals of Eisenstein series
19
.

In this work, we present essentially closed-form solutions for four of the six master integrals—
E_{1}(2;t)
,
E_{2}(2;t)
,
E_{3}(2;t)
and
\bar{E}_{4}(4;t)
—by extending and practically implementing the formulation of
E_{1}(2;t)
in terms of elliptic trilogarithms evaluated at sixths roots of unity
5
.
While this still requires numerical calculation of this special case of elliptic polylogarithms, the speed and precision of this approach are orders of magnitude greater than those of an approach involving numerical integration.
For the remaining two master integrals—
\bar{E}_{5}(4;t)
and
\bar{E}_{6}(4;t)
—we present an implementation in terms of contour integrals over
E_{1}(2;t)
.
Although it falls short of a closed-form solution, this specialized implementation offers much higher precision and stability than general-purpose codes while maintaining speed comparable to
pySecDec
.
All our results are valid for arbitrary complex values of the photon virtuality (with occasional exceptions near zero or at the two- and four-pion thresholds) and have numerical precision comfortably larger than that required by experiment.

E_{1}(2;t)
is the three-loop all-equal-masses sunset integral, and has been extensively studied.
For real virtualities below threshold, it has a Bessel integral representation
24
;
2
;
5
, and for all virtualities, it has the aforementioned one in terms of elliptic trilogarithms
5
.
These are natural functions of a nome
q
instead of the photon virtuality
t
,

1
1

1

Note the difference between the unphysical nome, for which
q
is the standard symbol, and the photon momentum, which is also commonly called
q
.
To reduce the potential for confusion, here we call the photon momentum
p
and mainly express it in terms of the photon virtuality in units of the pion mass,
t\coloneq p^{2}/M^{2}
.

so one major component of our extension of ref.
5
is the reliable mapping from
t
to
q
.
Another is the straightforward implementation of
E_{2}(2;t)
and
E_{3}(2;t)
, which relate to
E_{1}(2;t)
via differentiation and therefore involve elliptic polylogarithms of lower weight.

\bar{E}_{4}(4;t)
is then simply a linear combination of these and non-elliptic integrals.
Lastly, we express
\bar{E}_{5}(4;t)
and
\bar{E}_{6}(4;t)
as integrals of
E_{1}(2;t)
, indicating that these would be expressible in terms of elliptic polylogarithms of higher weight, although we are unable to find this expression and therefore resort to numerical integration.

The remainder of this paper is organised as follows.
In
section

2
we recall the definition of the master integrals that we will evaluate numerically.
In
section

3
we give the expressions of the masters
E_{1,2,3}
in terms of elliptic polylogarithms as the sixth root of unity, and provide two alternative formulations of these polylogarithms in terms of Eisenstein series with a mod 6 character.
We also discuss the properties of the map between the photon virtuality
t
and the nome
q
, needed for the numerical evaluation.

Section

4
is devoted to the evaluation of the masters
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
, which involves integrals over the master
E_{1}
.
Our results for the numerical evaluation of the master integrals and the vacuum polarization are given in
section

5
, and we conclude with
section

6
.
In
appendix

A
we give the small-
t
expansions of the elliptic master integrals.

2
Definitions

Here, we briefly restate the notation used for the integrals in
33
, so that we may define the objects of interest.
Letting
p
be the momentum of the external photon, with
t\coloneq p^{2}/M^{2}
its virtuality normalized to the pion mass
M
,
2
2

2

Since ref.
33
assumes the isospin limit, there is no mass difference between charged and neutral pions.
The common pion mass is normally taken as
M\approx 135\penalty\ \mathrm{MeV}
.

we choose the basis of loop momenta

\{k_{j}\}_{j=1}^{9}=\left\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\quad\ell_{1}-p,\ell_{2}-p,\ell_{3}-p,\quad\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}\right\}

(1)

so that all three-loop integrals in
d
dimensions (with
d
an integer minus
2\epsilon
) may be written in the form

\big[e^{\gamma_{E}}M^{2}\big]^{-3\epsilon}I_{\vec{\nu}}^{(d)}(t;M^{2})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{1}\;\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{2}\;\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{3}}{(2\pi)^{3d/2}}\prod_{j=1}^{9}\frac{1}{\big(k_{j}^{2}-M^{2}+i\varepsilon\big)^{\nu_{j}}}\,,

(2)

where
\vec{\nu}\in\mathbb{Z}^{9}
determines which combination of propagators appears in the integral, and where the normalization factor (involving the Euler-Mascheroni constant
\gamma_{E}
) ensures that the integral has integer mass dimension.

Of the
11
three-loop master integrals appearing in
33
(as determined through the Laporta algorithm
29
implemented in
LiteRed 2

31
;
32
), four can be written as products of one-loop integrals, allowing them to be expressed entirely in terms of the bubble functions

The remaining seven master integrals are

\displaystyle E_{0}(d)

\displaystyle\coloneq M^{2}I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,0,0,0,1,1,0}(p^{2},M^{2})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle E_{4}(d;t)

\displaystyle\coloneq M^{4}I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,1,0,0,1,1,0}(p^{2},M^{2})\,,

(5)

\displaystyle E_{1}(d;t)

\displaystyle\coloneq M^{2}I^{(d)}_{1,0,0,0,1,0,1,1,0}(p^{2},M^{2})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle E_{5}(d;t)

\displaystyle\coloneq M^{6}I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,1,1,0,1,1,0}(p^{2},M^{2})\,,

\displaystyle E_{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle\coloneq M^{4}I^{(d)}_{2,0,0,0,1,0,1,1,0}(p^{2},M^{2})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle E_{6}(d;t)

\displaystyle\coloneq M^{8}I^{(d)}_{2,1,0,1,1,0,1,1,0}(p^{2},M^{2})\,,

\displaystyle E_{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle\coloneq M^{6}I^{(d)}_{3,0,0,0,1,0,1,1,0}(p^{2},M^{2})\,.

Here, additional factors of
M
have been inserted to make the
E_{i}(2;t)
dimensionless.
We state most of these integrals in terms of their finite two-dimensional forms, to which they are related through Tarasov’s dimension-shifting formula
40
.
They are divergent in four dimensions, so there we use the finite part
\bar{E}_{i}(4;t)
, which we define to be dimensionless by including a factor of
M^{-6}
.

Of the masters, the three-loop sunset integral
E_{1}
is the most well-studied; see, e.g., refs.
23
;
2
;
5
;
38
;
18
.
In two dimensions, it satisfies the differential equation
3
3

3

(
33
, eqs. (A.4–5))

d=2

(6)

with polynomial coefficients

\displaystyle\pi_{13}(t)

\displaystyle=(t-16)(t-4)t^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\pi_{12}(t)

\displaystyle=6t(t^{2}-15t+32)\,,

(7)

\displaystyle\pi_{11}(t)

\displaystyle=7t^{2}-68t+64\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\pi_{10}(t)

\displaystyle=t-4\,,

on which we base our calculations, as described in

3

All other

.
Trivially,
E_{0}(d)=E_{1}(d;0)

\zeta(z)

4

.

3
The evaluation of
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
in two dimensions

In
(
33
, app. D)
, we stated the Bessel representation of these integrals
24
;
2
:

\displaystyle E_{1}(2;t)

\displaystyle=-8\int_{0}^{\infty}xI_{0}(x\sqrt{t})\big[K_{0}(x)\big]^{4}\mathrm{d}x\,,

(11)

\displaystyle E_{2}(2;t)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}x\Big\{I_{0}(x\sqrt{t})+\tfrac{x\sqrt{t}}{2}I_{1}(x\sqrt{t})\Big\}\big[K_{0}(x)\big]^{4}\mathrm{d}x\,,

(12)

\displaystyle E_{3}(2;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}x\Big\{(2+x^{2})I_{0}(x\sqrt{t})+\tfrac{x(t+2)}{\sqrt{t}}I_{1}(x\sqrt{t})+x^{2}I_{2}(x\sqrt{t})\Big\}\big[K_{0}(x)\big]^{4}\mathrm{d}x\,,

(13)

where
I_{\alpha}(z)
and
K_{\alpha}(z)
are the modified Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively.
While this representation is straightforward to write down, it is numerically costly and not valid everywhere.
Here, we will describe other, more powerful formulations due to Bloch, Kerr & Vanhove
5
that make manifest the elliptic nature of this integral.
Thus, this section, along with
section

3.4
, is a summary of the results of ref.
5
, extended with the related formulae for
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
, and with consideration given to the practical numerical evaluation of the integrals.

3.1
Elliptic polylogarithms

A natural variable for expressing the elliptic integral
E_{1}(2;t)
is the
nome

q\coloneq e^{2\pi i\tau}
, where
\Im(\tau)>0
.
It is related to our momentum parameter
t
through the (mirror) map

t(q)=-\bigg[\frac{\eta(q)\eta(q^{3})}{\eta(q^{2})\eta(q^{6})}\bigg]^{6}\,,

(14)

where
\eta(q)
is the Dedekind eta function defined by
5
5

5

In many contexts, including our numerical implementation,
\eta
is expressed as a function of
\tau
.
With this alternative definition,
t(\tau)=-\big[\eta(\tau)\eta(3\tau)/\eta(2\tau)\eta(6\tau)\big]^{6}
.

\eta(q)\coloneq q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(1-q^{n}\big)\,.

(15)

We will return to the relationship between
t
and
q
(or
\tau
) in
section

3.4
, but one thing to note is that since
|q|<1
, series involving
q
tend to converge rapidly regardless of what
t
is.

As shown in ref.
5
,
E_{1}(2;t)
is a period integral associated with a
K3
surface defined by the singular locus of the Feynman integrand. A detailed description of this connection
is beyond the scope of this work, but the essence is that
E_{1}(2;t)
connects deeply with the geometry of that surface, endowing it with a well-behaved, relatively simple
form that is closed up to a rapidly convergent sum.
6
6

6

While we focus on the
d=2
value here, there is recent work on higher-order terms in the
\epsilon
expansion
38
;
18
that appear in
d=4
; other recent work on this family of integrals includes refs.
17
;
20
;
16
;
37
.

Still following ref.
5
,
E_{1}(2;t)
depends on
q
through the
period

\varpi_{1}(q)\coloneq\frac{\big[\eta(q^{2})\eta(q^{6})\big]^{4}}{\big[\eta(q)\eta(q^{3})\big]^{2}}

(16)

and the weight-
r

elliptic polylogarithms

\eLi_{r}(q,z)
with

\displaystyle\eLi_{3}(q,z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\qquad\Li_{3}(z)

\displaystyle+\smash{\sum_{n\geq 1}}\Big[\Li_{3}\big(q^{n}z\big)+\Li_{3}\big(q^{n}z^{-1}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82234pt+\tfrac{1}{12}(\log z)^{3}-\tfrac{1}{24}\log q\,(\log z)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{720}(\log q)^{3}\,,

(17)

\displaystyle\eLi_{2}(q,z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}i_{3}(q,z)}{\partial\log q}

\displaystyle=\smash{\sum_{n\geq 1}}n\Big[\Li_{2}\big(q^{n}z\big)+\Li_{2}\big(q^{n}z^{-1}\big)\Big]-\tfrac{1}{24}(\log z)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{240}(\log q)^{2}\,,

(18)

\displaystyle\eLi_{1}(q,z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}i_{2}(q,z)}{\partial\log q}

\displaystyle=\smash{\sum_{n\geq 1}}n^{2}\Big[\Li_{1}\big(q^{n}z\big)+\Li_{1}\big(q^{n}z^{-1}\big)\Big]+\tfrac{1}{120}\log q\,,

(19)

of which we need the linear combination

\mathcal{H}_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to6.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-3.01685pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.9 0 L 6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{(r)}(q)\coloneq 24\eLi_{r}(q,\zeta_{6})+21\eLi_{r}(q,\zeta_{6}^{2})+8\eLi_{r}(q,\zeta_{6}^{3})+7\eLi_{r}(q,1)\,,

(20)

where
\zeta_{6}\coloneq\exp(i\pi/3)
is the principal sixth root of unity.
Then

\displaystyle E_{1}(2;t)

\displaystyle=-\varpi_{1}(q)\Big(40\pi^{2}\log q-48\mathcal{H}_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to6.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-3.01685pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{(1)}(q)}{\mathcal{D}_{\hskip-1.50694ptq}[t]^{2}}\,,

(26)

which are our main formulae for
E_{2}(2;t)
and
E_{3}(2;t)
.
With these in hand, the integrals may be computed over the entire
q
-disk, as shown in
fig.

1
.

Figure 1
:
The integrals
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
(left to right), shown as functions of the nome
q
over the complex unit disk.
This disk corresponds to a fractal tiling of infinitely many copies of the complex
t
-plane (see
fig.

3
), and we outline one such copy in white here.
For each integral, its value at
t=0
(limit as
q\to 1
) has been subtracted to make the features more visible.
The integral values are shown using domain coloring: the hue represents their phase (with
red
and
cyan
for positive and negative real values, respectively), while the contours represent their absolute value. The latter is a power of
2
on the contours, and decreases toward the shaded side.
As a reference for reading the contours, the top corner of the white outline (corresponding to
t=4
) has
0.5<|E_{1}(2;4)-E_{1}(2;0)|<1
,
0.25<|E_{2}(2;4)-E_{2}(2;0)|<0.5
, and
0.125<|E_{3}(2;4)-E_{3}(2;0)|<0.25
.
Note the branch cut on
q\in[-1,0]
, which corresponds to two copies of
t\in[16,\infty)
(see
fig.

3
).

3.2
Eisenstein series with a character

While the sums in
sections

3.1
,
18
, and
19
converge well, the functions of
q
used here possess a form that is even better suited for numerical evaluation.
This involves
Eisenstein series
, which we express as
7
7

7

This notation is
ad hoc
, with the conventional Eisenstein series being (for
k\geq 2
)

E_{2k}(\tau)\coloneq\frac{1}{2\zeta(2k)}\sum_{(m,n)\neq(0,0)}\frac{1}{(m+n\tau)^{2k}}=1+\frac{2\E(1-2k,1;q)}{\zeta(1-2k)}\,.

\E(a,b;q)\coloneq\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n^{a}}\frac{q^{n}}{(1-q^{n})^{b}}\,.

(27)

Such series converge rapidly.
To compactly represent linear combinations of the form
\sum_{k\in K}c_{k}\E(a,b;q^{k})
where
K
and
\{c_{k}\}_{k\in K}
are sets of integers, we use

\E(a,b,\psi;q)\coloneq\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\psi(n)}{n^{a}}\frac{q^{n}}{(1-q^{n})^{b}}\,,\qquad\psi(n)\coloneq\sum_{k\in K}\begin{cases}c_{k}k^{a}&\text{if $k$ divides $n$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(28)

where the
character

\psi(n)
is periodic with a period equal to the least common multiple of
K
.
All cases appearing here have period 6:
\psi(n+6)=\psi(n)
.

As proven in ref.
5
,
8
8

8

While deriving either form of
\mathcal{H}_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to6.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-3.01685pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

Other examples, easily obtainable from the definitions, are

\displaystyle\log\eta(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{\log q}{24}-\E(1,1;q)\,,

(30)

\displaystyle\log[-t(q)]

\displaystyle=-\log q-6\E(1,1,\psi_{t};q)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\{\psi_{t}(n)\}_{n=1}^{6}

\displaystyle=\{1,-1,4,-1,1,-4\}\,,

\displaystyle\log\varpi_{1}(q)

\displaystyle=\log q+2\E(1,1,\psi_{\varpi};q)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\{\psi_{\varpi}(n)\}_{n=1}^{6}

\displaystyle=\{1,-3,4,-3,1,-12\}\,.

Derivatives are straightforwardly obtained through

\frac{\partial\E(a,b,\psi;q)}{\partial\log q}=b\E(a-1,b+1,\psi;q)+(1-b)\E(a-1,b,\psi;q)\,,

(31)

which readily provides

\mathcal{D}_{\hskip-1.50694ptq}[t]=-1-6\E(0,2,\psi_{t};q)\,,\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\hskip-1.50694ptq}[\varpi_{1}]=1+2\E(0,2,\psi_{\varpi};q)\,,

(32)

etc., as needed for efficient evaluation of
E_{2}(2;t)
and
E_{3}(2;t)
via
eqs.

25
and
3.1
.
Furthermore, from
eq.

(34)

Closed-form expressions sometimes exist.
For instance, using

(
35
, sequence A125510)
we can express
\mathcal{D}_{\hskip-1.50694ptq}[t]
as a sum of
\eta
-ratios:

\mathcal{D}_{\hskip-1.50694ptq}[t]=-\frac{\big[\eta(q)\eta(q^{2})\big]^{4}+9\big[\eta(q^{3})\eta(q^{6})\big]^{4}}{\eta(q)\eta(q^{2})\eta(q^{3})\eta(q^{6})}\,.

(35)

3.3
Poisson resummation

The formulae provided above converge poorly near
t=0
, corresponding to
q
near
1
.
While the series expansions of the integrals (
appendix

A
) are accurate in this limit, it is also possible to improve the behavior of the elliptic formulation through Poisson resummation.
The steps follow ref.
(
5
, §2.4)
.

Compared to the other formulations, this is numerically better behaved as
\tau\to 0
.
Although
e^{-i\pi\frac{\hat{n}}{3\tau}}
has an essential singularity at
\tau=0
, its pathological behavior does not materialize when
\tau
approaches zero along the imaginary axis, which corresponds to
t
approaching zero from below (see
fig.

2
).
When
t
approaches zero from above,
\tau
approaches zero along a circular arc tangent to the imaginary axis, which puts it close enough to the axis that the behavior remains non-pathological.

3.4
Obtaining
q
and
\tau
from
t

While the expressions for
E_{i}(2;t)
presented above are analytically and numerically well-behaved,
they are given in terms of
q
, whose complicated relation
eq.

14
with the kinematic variable
t
means it is not
a priori
clear how to evaluate them for a given value of
t
.
In this section, aided by
figs.

2
and
3
, we aim to clarify that relationship, allowing for the direct evaluation of
E_{1}(2;t)
anywhere in the complex
t
-plane.

Figure 2
:
Left
: the mapping
\tau\mapsto t
visualized in the complex plane, covering one period in the real direction.
The complex values are represented as in
fig.

1
.
The
\times
-shaped contour intersections are saddle points at
t=4
(for instance at
\tau=\pm\frac{1}{4}+\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{12}
) and
t=16
(for instance at
\tau=\pm\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{6}
).

Right
: a sketch (to scale) of the
\Re(\tau)>0
half of the same plot, showing a selection of the infinitely many
\tau
-plane images of the real
t
-axis, colored according to which kinematic region they reproduce: spacelike subthreshold (
t<0
,
blue
), timelike subthreshold (
0<t<4
,
orange
), two-pion (
4<t<16
,
green
) and multi-pion (
16<t
,
violet
).
The shaded area (if extended upward to infinity) contains all
t
with
\Im(t)>0
exactly once, with contours indicating (bottom to top)
\Im(t)=0.001
,
0.1
,
0.5
, and
1
; its boundary (bold) contains all real
t
exactly once, ordered counterlockwise.

Figure 3
:
Like
fig.

2
, but showing
q\mapsto t
over the unit disk.
The lines in the sketch have been extended onto the
\Re(\tau)<0
part (lower half of the disk), and thereby produce the outline used in
fig.

1
.
Note how inside the shaded region,
q
approaches
1
extremely slowly as
|t|
approaches
0
, ensuring good convergence of our sums almost everywhere.

The nome
q
lives in the unit disk
|q|<1
, and its parameter
\tau
lives in the upper half-plane
\Im(\tau)>0
.
The mapping

\tau\mapsto q=e^{2\pi i\tau}

maps the real axis to the boundary of the disk, and
+i\infty
to its center.
It has period
1
in the real direction, so
\tau
can be restricted to the strip
-\frac{1}{2}<\Re(\tau)\leq\frac{1}{2}
without loss of generality.
With this restriction, the mapping is one-to-one.
The presence of
\log q=2\pi i\tau
in
E_{1}(2;t)
makes it sensitive to
\tau\mapsto\tau+n
.
This is as expected: because
E_{1}(2;t)
is a Feynman integral, it should not be a modular form (which would be invariant under
\tau\mapsto\tau+n
) but a multivalued function whose monodromy (discontinuity across its branch cut) corresponds precisely to
\tau\mapsto\tau\pm 1
.

The mapping

q\mapsto t

[i.e.,
eq.

14
]
is ill-behaved near any point on the rim of the disk where every possible value of
t
(except
0
, which never occurs) appears infinitely many times.
On the other hand,
t=0
is obtained without problem in, e.g., the limit
q\to 1
along the real axis (
\tau\to 0
along the imaginary axis).
Every nonzero value of
t
appears infinitely often in the image of the upper half-plane under

q\mapsto t
,
as illustrated in the fractal-like structure of
figs.

2
and
3
.
In these figures, we have indicated a region
that contains each
t
with
\Im(t)>0
exactly once,
and whose boundary is a line (bold) corresponding to the real
t
-axis.

The inverse mapping

t\mapsto q

(or rather
t\mapsto\tau
)
is described in ref.
(
5
, §3.2)
.
However, it involves several multivalued functions (denoted
\rho
below) which, if restricted to their respective principal branches, fail to produce the correct result: it is entirely wrong for
-0.12\lesssim t\lesssim 0.2
and correct only up to a phase (a piecewise constant power of
i
) elsewhere.
Here, we correct the branch choices so that the mapping
t\mapsto\tau
is analytic everywhere except for a branch cut on the real line.
9
9

9

This procedure of correcting
f(g(z))
, where
f
is multivalued and
g
non-monotonous, by supplanting
f
with information about
z
, can be thought of as analogous to how
\mathrm{atan2}(y,x)
(as implemented by most programming languages) is the correct way of computing
\arg(x+iy)
, whereas
\tan^{-1}(y/x)
may be wrong by a multiple of
\pi
.

The mapping is, as shown in ref.
6
, given by a ratio of periods,
10
10

10

The factor of
i
is strictly speaking not part of the relation, and can be absorbed by appropriately redefining the
\rho
functions below.
However, placing it here allows us to make their definition the same for
\Im(t)>0
and
\Im(t)<0
, simplifying the expressions.

However, an appropriate, nontrivial choice of branch must be made for these multivalued functions to obtain the correct mapping
t\mapsto\tau
.
We will therefore replace them by canonical versions that are analytic and single-valued when restricted to a subset
\Omega
of the complex plane, such that there is a one-to-one mapping between this
\Omega
and the entire
t

to a separate copy of
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)
.
11
11

11

Note that we exclude
[0,\infty)
from the
t
-plane, even though the branch cuts of the master integrals only start at
t=4
or
t=16
.
This is partly because we expect a discontinuous
t\mapsto\tau
map at
t\in[0,16]
—compare
fig.

2
, where these parts of the boundary of the
\Im(t)>0
region are not adjacent to their
\Im(t)<0
counterparts—and partly because it is not possible to make the mapping analytic on more inclusive sets like
\mathbb{C}\setminus[4,\infty)
or
\mathbb{C}\setminus[16,\infty)
.
As can be seen in
fig.

5
,
\rho_{4}
will inevitably have a branch cut on the parts of the real line corresponding to either
t<0
or
0<t<4
(or somewhere worse), and as can be seen in
fig.

4
, the step from
t+i\epsilon
to
t-i\epsilon
for
t\in(4,16)
is not local in
\Omega_{>}
.

The same is true in the context of
\varpi_{r}
, since
z\mapsto\frac{9z-9}{z-9}
maps each of
\Omega_{\gtrless}
to itself.
However,
\rho_{4}
and
\rho_{F}
possess branch points at the complex roots
z\approx 0.15\pm 0.57i
of
z^{3}-9z^{2}+3z-3
, which are in
\Omega_{<}
and therefore make it impossible to construct single-valued versions there.
The real root
z\approx 8.69
is also a branch point, but is not contained in
\Omega_{>}
and therefore poses no problem.
Thus, we will focus our construction on
\Omega_{>}
.
12
12

12

\Omega_{>}
has the downside of not being bounded, with

corresponding to

, but this is a small price to pay for single-valuedness.

such that we map onto
\Omega_{>}
.
From
eq.

42
,

t_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to6.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-3.01685pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\frac{5t-32+4\rho_{2}(t)}{t}\,,

(47)

where
\rho_{2}
is nominally equal to
\sqrt{(z-4)(z-16)}
, but to obtain the correct mapping, we must make the correct choice of branch (i.e., sign in front of the square root).
Because
\sqrt{z}
has a branch cut along the negative real axis,
\rho_{2}(z)

has a branch cut at
z\in[4,16]
, which we retain, and one along
\Re(z)=10
, which we remove through (see
fig.

5
):

\rho_{2}(z)\coloneq\sqrt{(z-4)(z-16)}\times\begin{cases}-1&\text{if $\Re(z)<10$},\\
+1&\text{if $\Re(z)>10$}.\\
\end{cases}

(48)

This makes
\rho_{2}(t)
analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[4,\infty)
and makes
eq.

47
map
t
onto
\Omega_{>}
; the opposite sign choice would map it onto the undesirable
\Omega_{<}
.

Figure 5
:

The construction of
\rho_{2}(z)
(top row) and
\rho_{4}(z)
(bottom row).

Left
: The principal branch of
\sqrt{(z-4)(z-16)}
and
\sqrt[4]{z^{3}-9z^{2}+3z-3}
, respectively, visualized in the complex plane using colors and contours like in
fig.

2
.

Middle
: The corrected functions
\rho_{2}(z)
and
\rho_{4}(z)
, respectively.

Right
: A schematic picture of their relation.
Colored lines indicate where the argument of the root [
(z-4)(z-16)
and
z^{3}-9z^{2}+3z-3
, respectively] is real:
red
for positive values and
cyan
for negative.
Dotted lines do not cause branch cuts.
Solid lines cause cuts that are removed by analytically continuing to different branches, starting on the principal branch in the region marked by
\star
and picking up roots of unity (
-1
and
+i
, respectively) when crossing a line in the direction indicated by the arrows.
Zigzag lines are left as branch cuts also in
\rho_{2}(z)
or
\rho_{4}(z)
.
The dotted circle indicates the boundary between
\Omega_{<}
and
\Omega_{>}
(compare
fig.

4
).

Now we can turn to
\rho_{4}(z)
.
Its branch cuts happen when
\Im(z^{3}-9t^{2}+3t-3)
changes sign, which happens on the real axis and on the hyperbola
\{x+iy\,|\,y^{2}=x^{2}-18x+3\}
(see
fig.

5
).
Of course, there is only a branch cut on the segments of these curves where
\Re(z^{3}-9t^{2}+3t-3)<0
, but by assigning different phases to each of the six regions into which these curves divide the complex planes, we can change along which segments the discontinuities appear in practice.
If done appropriately, this gives a result that is analytic and single-valued on
\Omega_{>}
.
Of course,
\rho_{4}(z)
is not uniquely determined by this condition, but the global phase can rather easily be fixed by seeing which of the four possibilities gives the correct
t\mapsto\tau
.
The appropriate choice is

\rho_{4}(z)\coloneq\sqrt[4]{z^{3}-9z^{2}+3z-3}\times\begin{cases}+i&\text{if $\Im(z^{3}-9z^{2}+3z-3)<0$ and $\Re(z)>3$,}\\
-1&\text{if $\Im(z)>0$,}\\
+1&\text{if $\Im(z)<0$,}\end{cases}

(49)

where the complicated first condition means “to the right of the hyperbola”.
This gives
\rho_{4}(z)
branch cuts along the real axis below the real root of
z^{3}-9z^{2}+3z-3
, and between the complex roots along the hyperbola (see
fig.

5
).

Lastly, we have
\rho_{F}(z)
.
Looking at
eq.

45
,
F_{1}(z)
has a branch cut along
(1,\infty)
, producing numerous discontinuities, and to treat it like we just treated
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{4}
, we must first determine its monodromy (behavior when moving between branches), which is less simple than just picking up phases.
Helpful formulae for this task are found in
refs.
3
;
30
.

F_{1}(z)
is a solution of the hypergeometric differential equation
(
30
, §9.5(9.5.4))
with
(a,b,c)=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{12}},\nicefrac{{5}}{{12}},1)
,

0=144z(z-1)f^{\prime\prime}(z)+\left(216z-144\right)f^{\prime}(z)+5f(z)\,,

(50)

which has as its other independent solution
(
30
, §9.5(9.5.6))

F_{2}(z)\coloneq{}_{2\hskip-1.0pt}\mathit{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt1}\bigg(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\sfrac 1{12},\sfrac 5{12}}{\sfrac 12}\bigg|1-z\bigg)\,.

(51)

Since
{}_{2\hskip-1.0pt}\mathit{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt1}(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{c}|z)
has a branch cut along
z\in[1,\infty)
,
F_{1}(z)
has the same cut whereas
F_{2}(z)
has its cut along
z\in(-\infty,0]
.
Letting
C_{x}
be the clockwise circular contour of radius
1/2
centered on
z=x
, we will therefore consider the behavior of
F_{1}(z)
and
F_{2}(z)
when analytically continuing around
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
, i.e., their monodromy around the points
0
and
1
, respectively.
This is illustrated in
fig.

6
.

Figure 6
:
The contours used in the monodromy construction, with cuts drawn as zigzag lines for the different functions involved.

Left
:
F_{1}(z)
, cut on
[1,\infty)
.

Middle
:
F_{2}(z)
, cut on
(-\infty,0]
.

Right
:
{}_{2\hskip-1.0pt}\mathit{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt1}(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{c}|\frac{1}{z})
, showing how the combination of
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
results in a contour that encircles the cut without crossing it.

The monodromies are a linear transformation in the space
spanned by
F_{1}(z)
and
F_{2}(z)
:

\displaystyle F_{1}(z)

\displaystyle\overset{C_{0}}{\longrightarrow}\alpha F_{1}(z)+\beta F_{2}(z)\,,\qquad F_{2}(z)\overset{C_{0}}{\longrightarrow}\gamma F_{1}(z)+\delta F_{2}(z)\,,

(52)

\displaystyle F_{1}(z)

\displaystyle\overset{C_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\tilde{\alpha}F_{1}(z)+\tilde{\beta}F_{2}(z)\,,\qquad F_{2}(z)\overset{C_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\tilde{\gamma}F_{1}(z)+\tilde{\delta}F_{2}(z)\,,

(53)

where the constants
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
are determined by the local behavior of
the functions
F_{1}(z)
and
F_{2}(z)
near
z=0
, and the constants

\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta},\tilde{\gamma},\tilde{\delta}
by the local behavior near
z=1
.

Following the Frobenius method (see, e.g., ref.
(
28
, XVI(§16.1))
),
eq.

50
has one analytic solution in the vicinity of
0
as well as one containing
\log(z)
.
Since
F_{1}(z)
has no branch cut near
0
, the logarithmic component must be entirely contained within
F_{2}(z)
, and working out the solution by series expansion (as is done explicitly in ref.
(
30
, §9.7(9.7.5))
), we find

F_{2}(z)\overset{z\sim 0}{=}-\frac{\log(z)}{4\pi}\frac{F_{1}(z)}{F_{1}(1)}+\text{(analytic terms)}\,.

(54)

Continuing along
C_{0}
results in
\log(z)\to\log(z)+2\pi i
, so the
C_{0}
monodromy is the linear transformation
13
13

13

This can also be seen from the identity
(
3
, §2.10(1))

F_{1}(z)=\frac{\Gamma(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})}{\Gamma(\nicefrac{{11}}{{12}})\Gamma(\nicefrac{{7}}{{12}})}F_{2}(z)+\frac{\Gamma(\nicefrac{{{-1}}}{{2}})}{\Gamma(\nicefrac{{1}}{{12}})\Gamma(\nicefrac{{5}}{{12}})}F_{3}(z)\,,

where
F_{3}(z)\coloneq\sqrt{1-z}\penalty\ {}_{2\hskip-1.0pt}\mathit{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt1}\big(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\sfrac{11}{12},\sfrac{7}{12}}{\sfrac 32}\big|1-z\big)

and, incidentally, the coefficient in front of
F_{2}(z)
is equal to
F_{1}(1)

(
3
, §2.8(46))
.
The right-hand side is unchanged by
C_{1}
except that the square root picks up a minus sign, so eliminating
F_{3}(z)
, we find
eq.

57
.

F_{1}(z)\overset{C_{0}}{\longrightarrow}F_{1}(z)\,,\qquad F_{2}(z)\overset{C_{0}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}(z)-\frac{iF_{1}(z)}{2F_{1}(1)}\,.

(55)

Note that repeating
C_{0}
produces an infinite tower of branches, although none of these is directly accessible from the principal sheet of
\rho_{F}(z)
, which is purely
F_{1}(z)
and therefore unaffected by
C_{0}
.

:

:

\rho_{4}(z)
in

.

makes the features more visible.

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -3.9 0 L -6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.9 0 L 6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

, which holds within the
orange
area.
The monodromies described in the text are used when crossing the solid lines to keep
\rho_{F}(z)
continuous:
C_{0}
(
\rightarrow
) or
C_{1}
(
\leftrightarrow
) is applied when going in the direction of the arrows (recall that
C_{1}
is involutory, hence the bidirectional arrow).

Again, following the Frobenius method,

eq.

50
has one analytic solution in the vicinity of
1
as well as one containing
\sqrt{1-z}
.
Since
F_{2}(z)
has no branch cut near
1
, the square root component must be contained within
F_{1}(z)
, and through series expansion (done explicitly in ref.
(
30
, §9.7(9.7.1))
) we find

F_{1}(z)\overset{z\sim 1}{=}F_{1}(1)F_{2}(z)+\sqrt{1-z}\times\text{(analytic terms)}\,,

(56)

which also follows from ref.
(
30
, §9.7(9.7.1))
.
The effect of
C_{1}
is to change the sign of the square root, so eliminating the analytic terms, we find

F_{1}(z)\overset{C_{1}}{\longrightarrow}2F_{1}(1)F_{2}(z)-F_{1}(z)\,,\qquad F_{2}(z)\overset{C_{1}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}(z)\,.

(57)

Note that, unlike
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
, only alternates between two branches:
C_{1}^{2}=1
.

With the monodromy in hand, we can construct
\rho_{F}(z)
similarly to
\rho_{2}(z)
and
\rho_{4}(z)
, as illustrated in
fig.

7
.
An explicit result in the style of
eqs.

48
and
49
would be long and not very illuminating, so we defer it to the attached code
39
.
It has a branch cut along
(-\infty,9)
, and like
\rho_{4}(z)
, it also has one between the complex roots of
z^{3}-9z^{2}+3z-3
, this time following the leftmost bend of the cubic curve
\{x+iy\,|\,y^{2}(4-x)=x^{3}-6x^{2}+9x\}
.
14
14

14

If one crosses this cut with a
C_{0}
monodromy, one recovers the principal branch times
-i
.
This is because
(C_{0}C_{1})^{3}=-i
, which can be understood from the identities
(
3
, §2.10(2–3))

that relate both
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
to various
{}_{2\hskip-1.0pt}\mathit{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt1}(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{c}|\frac{1}{z})
times
12
th roots.
Those hypergeometric functions only have a branch cut along
(0,1)
, so the combined
C_{0}C_{1}
contour (see
fig.

6
) is equivalent to a loop that encircles the entire cut without crossing it, only picking up phases from the
12
th roots.
After three laps around the combined contour, these yield a global factor of
-i
.
This can be used to derive
eq.

57
without the Frobenius method, and
can also be shown based on the integral representation of the hypergeometric function
(
43
, §4.2)
.

Going forward, we will use all of these simultaneously, so for clarity, we will
use
\langle x\mapsto y\rangle
as shorthand for the function
f:x\mapsto y

that maps variable
x

y
.
The concrete implementations of this function are summarized in
table

1
.

For future convenience, we will add yet another parameter,
\theta
, which parametrizes the line in
\tau
-space that corresponds to real
t
:

\langle\theta\mapsto\tau\rangle(\theta)=\begin{cases}-\frac{i\theta}{16}&\text{if }\theta<0\,,\\
\tfrac{1}{6}+\tfrac{1}{6}e^{i\pi\frac{6-\theta}{6}}&\text{if }0\leq\theta<4\,,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{6}e^{i\pi\frac{34-\theta}{36}}&\text{if }4\leq\theta<16\,,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{i\theta\sqrt{3}}{96}&\text{if }16\leq\theta\,.\end{cases}

(58)

Composed with
\langle\tau\mapsto t\rangle
, this makes
\langle\theta\mapsto t\rangle
a monotonous, once continuously differentiable function with the property that
\langle\theta\mapsto t\rangle(\theta)=\theta
for
\theta\in\{0,4,16\}
.
This parametrization of the real
t
-axis makes up for the fact that our implementation of
\langle t\mapsto\tau\rangle
requires
\Im(t)>0
.

satisfies the differential equation
(

t=4/(1-\beta^{2})

\bar{E}_{5}(4;\beta)=c_{1}\,g_{1}(\beta)+c_{2}\,g_{2}(\beta)-g_{1}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{1}}^{\beta}\mathscr{S}_{5}(\xi)g_{2}(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}}+g_{2}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{2}}^{\beta}\mathscr{S}_{5}(\xi)g_{1}(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}}\,,

where, for convenience, we express the integrals as

\displaystyle-G_{1}\int_{\xi_{1}}^{\beta}\bigg[\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)+\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{rat}}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}g_{2}(\xi)+E_{1}(2;\xi)h_{2}(\xi)\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi

that is,

and

63
that
15
15

15

Note that the original solution,
eq.

61
, produces a sum of integrals similar to our
\mathcal{I}
, but which, if used as-is, would give the property
\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\mathcal{I}[\beta;g_{1}(\beta),g_{2}(\beta)]=\mathcal{I}[\beta;g^{\prime}_{1}(\beta),g^{\prime}_{2}(\beta)]
.
This does not hold here due to the integration by parts performed to eliminate derivatives of
E_{1}
in the integrand, resulting in the
\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}E_{1}(\beta)
term in
eq.

63
and the combination of
E_{1}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}E_{1}
in
section

4
.
The latter may be derived by noting that
\smash{\frac{t-16}{2t}}=\smash{\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}}
and that
\smash{\frac{t^{2}-28t+48}{3t^{2}(t-4)}}
is equal to either of

\frac{(\beta^{2}-1)^{2}}{8\beta}\bigg[\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}-g_{1}(\beta)h_{2}(\beta)+g_{2}(\beta)h_{1}(\beta)\bigg]=\frac{(\beta^{2}-1)^{2}}{8\beta}\big[g_{2}(\beta)g^{\prime}_{1}(\beta)-g^{\prime}_{2}(\beta)g_{1}(\beta)\big]\bigg[s_{1}(\beta)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}\bigg]\,,

where the second expression comes from applying the
t
-derivative
before
doing integration by parts.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{E}_{5}(4;t)}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{t-16}{2t}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{t^{2}-28t+48}{3t^{2}(t-4)}\bigg]E_{1}(2;t)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\frac{(\beta^{2}-1)^{2}}{8\beta}\Big(2\beta c_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}J_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to4.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-2.45348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}
{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{(1)}(\beta)\big]\Big)\,,

(68)

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the numerically practical evaluation of
\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]
, and (in
section

4.2
) to the determination of the constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
in
eq.

63
.

4.1
The spacelike region

As shown in
figs.

2
,
3
, and
4
, the real
t
-line naturally decomposes into four regions, of which we now consider the spacelike one, i.e.,
t<0
or
\beta>1
.
We split the integrand of
eq.

64
into multiple pieces, some of which yield integrals that can be evaluated analytically and others that require numerical evaluation.
Importantly, we are free to choose different integration limits
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
separately for each piece.
We will treat the analytic pieces as indefinite integrals, leaving
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
entirely unspecified and compensating with our choice of
c_{1},c_{2}
.
For the integrals evaluated numerically, we choose
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\infty
, ensuring that the integrals converge, since this avoids dependence on arbitrarily chosen finite limits and allows use of the known
t\to 0
behavior of
\bar{E}_{5}
, namely
eq.

94
. Thus:

\bullet
The contribution from
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{rat}}
can be evaluated analytically, and we leave it as an indefinite integral.
16
16

16

In fact, since
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{rat}}(\beta)g_{1}(\beta)/\beta^{2}
does not vanish as
\beta\to\infty
, it would be inconvenient to write it as a definite integral, since the only conveniently accessible limit,
\xi_{2}=\infty
, is excluded.
Below, we encounter (and solve) the same problem for the contributions involving
E_{1}
, but we can avoid it altogether thanks to the integral’s analytic solvability.

Writing
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(n)}(\beta)\coloneq\int\frac{\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{rat}}(\beta)g_{n}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}\mathrm{d}\beta
, we choose the primitive functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(1)}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}-8}{2}\log\bigg(\frac{\beta+1}{\beta-1}\bigg)+\frac{24+\big[68+4\zeta(3)-\pi^{2}\big]\beta^{2}}{6\beta}\,,

(69)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(2)}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{\pi^{2}-8}{16}\log\bigg(\frac{\beta+1}{\beta-1}\bigg)+\frac{24+\big[68+4\zeta(3)-\pi^{2}\big]\beta^{2}}{24\beta}\bigg]\log\bigg(\frac{\beta+1}{\beta-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\log\bigg(\frac{{\beta}^{2}-1}{{\beta}^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{\zeta(3)}{3}+\frac{8-\pi^{2}}{4(\beta^{2}-1)}+\frac{68-\pi^{2}+4\zeta(3)}{12}\,,

(70)

with constants of integration chosen so that their contribution to
\bar{E}_{5}(4;\beta)
is finite as
\beta\to\infty
[see
eq.

78a
].

\bullet
The contribution stemming from
\mathscr{S}_{J}
can be evaluated analytically as well: in terms of polylogarithm weight,
\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)
has weight
3
and
g_{2}(\xi)
has weight
1
, so the integral of their product has weight
5
. It is easily obtained using
hyperInt

36
.
However, the resulting expression is very long and involves generalized polylogarithms, the evaluation of which is no faster than evaluating the integral numerically.
Therefore, we do exactly this, numerically evaluating the convergent integrals

\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(n)}(\beta)\coloneq-\int_{\beta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)g_{n}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\,,

(71)

where we have set
\xi_{n}=\infty
and interchanged the limits.

\bullet
The contributions involving
E_{1}(2;\beta)
must be evaluated numerically.
From
eq.

66
, as
\xi\to\infty
,

h_{1}(\xi)=18\xi^{2}-1+\mathcal{O}(1/\xi^{2})\,,\qquad h_{2}(\xi)=\frac{9}{5\xi^{3}}+\mathcal{O}(1/\xi^{5})\,,

(72)

and from
eq.

94
,

E_{1}(2;\xi)=-7\zeta(3)+\frac{7\zeta(3)-6}{4\xi^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(1/\xi^{4})\,.

(73)

Therefore,
\int_{\beta}^{\infty}h_{2}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)
converges but
\int_{\beta}^{\infty}h_{1}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)
does not.
To remedy this, we subtract the leading large-
\xi
behavior of
E_{1}(2;\xi)
, writing
h_{1}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)=\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\xi)+\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{div}}(\xi)
with

\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{div}}(\xi)\coloneq h_{1}(\xi)\bigg(-7\zeta(3)+\frac{7\zeta(3)-6}{4\xi^{2}}\bigg)\,,\qquad\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\xi)\coloneq h_{1}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)-\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{div}}(\xi)\,.

(74)

Now, the divergent integral can be evaluated analytically,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)\coloneq\int\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle=-42\zeta(3)\beta^{3}+{77\zeta(3)-54\over 2}\beta+\frac{203\zeta(3)-30}{12\beta}+\frac{6-7\zeta(3)}{6\beta^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{7\zeta(3)+2}{4}\log\bigg(\frac{\beta+1}{\beta-1}\bigg)\,,

(75)

leaving the convergent numerical integrals

\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(1)}(\beta)\coloneq-\int_{\beta}^{\infty}\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\,,\qquad\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(2)}(\beta)\coloneq-\int_{\beta}^{\infty}h_{2}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)\,.

(76)

Summing up the contributions from
eqs.

69
,
71
,
4.1
, and
76
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]

\displaystyle=G_{2}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(1)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(1)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(1)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)\Big]

\displaystyle-G_{1}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(2)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(2)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(2)}(\beta)\Big]\,,

(77)

which can be plugged into
eq.

63
.

4.2
Constants of integration

We can now determine
c_{1},c_{2}
in
eq.

63
by comparing
section

4.1
with the asymptotic behavior as
t
approaches
0
from below (i.e., as
\beta\to\infty
).
We can, in fact, directly see that
c_{2}=0
: all of
\bar{E}_{5}(4;\beta)
,
g_{1}(\beta)
and
\mathcal{I}[\beta;g_{1}(\beta),g_{2}(\beta)]
are even under
\beta\to-\beta
, but
g_{2}(\beta)
is odd.
To determine
c_{1}
, we use the expansion
g_{2}(\beta)=-\frac{1}{3\beta}-\frac{1}{15\beta^{3}}+\mathcal{O}(1/\beta^{5})
along with
eqs.

69
,
71
,
4.1
, and
76
, giving

\displaystyle g_{2}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(1)}(\beta)-g_{1}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(2)}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}-4\zeta(3)-68}{12}

\displaystyle+\frac{4-\pi^{2}}{4\beta^{2}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\beta^{4}}\Big)\,,

(78a)

\displaystyle g_{2}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(1)}(\beta)-g_{1}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(2)}(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phantom{+}\;\frac{\pi^{2}-4}{12\beta^{2}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\beta^{4}}\Big)\,,

(78b)

\displaystyle g_{2}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(1)}(\beta)-g_{1}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(2)}(\beta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{63\zeta(3)}{10}

\displaystyle+\frac{4698-1147\zeta 3}{1440\beta^{2}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\beta^{4}}\Big)\,,

(78c)

\displaystyle\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}E_{1}(2;\beta)+g_{2}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{180+119\zeta(3)}{30}

\displaystyle+\frac{4662-12713\zeta(3)}{1440\beta^{2}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\beta^{4}}\Big)\,,

(78d)

where we have cancelled the divergent part of
g_{2}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)=14\zeta(3)\beta^{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)
against that of the boundary term in
eq.

63
.
17
17

17

Note that the total
E_{1}
contribution is the simpler-looking

\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}E_{1}(2;\beta)+g_{2}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(1)}(\beta)-g_{1}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(2)}(\beta)-g_{1}(\beta)\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)=\frac{18-7\zeta(3)}{3}+\frac{52-77\zeta(3)}{8\beta^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\beta^{4}\big)\,.

This would appear more directly if the original expression
(
33
, eq. (C.26))
were used, but this complication is more than made up for by having only one elliptic function—namely
E_{1}(2;\xi)
, not its derivatives—in the integrand.

The absence of a
\beta^{2}
term in
eq.

78
immediately tells us that
c_{1}=0
, and summing up the contributions, we see that the total asymptotic behavior precisely matches the large-
\beta
expansions of
\bar{E}_{5}(4;\beta)
from
eq.

94
:

\bar{E}_{5}(4;\beta)=\frac{4+\pi^{2}-32\zeta(3)}{12}+\frac{105\zeta(3)-8\pi^{2}-34}{48\beta^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\beta^{4}\big)\,.

(79)

This equality holds to all orders in
1/\beta^{2}
, as is easily verified by extending the expansions above.
Since
c_{1}=c_{2}=0
, we will ignore the constants of integration from now on.

4.3
The timelike region, and everywhere else

Section

4.1
holds for
\beta>1
(i.e.,
t<0
), but in fact it remains valid when
0<t<4
, corresponding to
\beta=i\tilde{\beta}
with
\tilde{\beta}>0
.
This is seen by changing the integration variable to
\tilde{\beta}
(and, similarly, to
\tilde{\xi}
with
\xi=i\tilde{\xi}
).
In the cases relevant for
\bar{E}_{5}
and
\bar{E}_{6}
,

G_{1}
and the integrals multiplying it stay real, and
G_{2}
and those multiplying it become purely imaginary, as follows from
\log[(i\tilde{\xi}+1)/(i\tilde{\xi}-1)]=-2i\tan^{-1}(1/\tilde{\xi})
etc. All considerations about convergence carry over since
\beta=\infty
and
\tilde{\beta}=\infty
both correspond to
t=0
.
Thus,
eq.

63
holds for all
t
below the two-pion threshold at
t=4
.

However, we now demonstrate another method, which is equivalent to the above for
t<4
, but which also generalizes to the above-threshold region
t>4
, and indeed to the entire complex plane.

The above-threshold region (
t>4
) corresponds to
\beta\in(0,1)
, but here there is a branch cut along the real line requiring that
t
, and therefore
\beta
, be given a small imaginary part.
18
18

18

Note that, for the purposes of
\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]
, there is nothing special happening at the four-pion threshold (
t=16
,
\beta=\sqrt{3}/2
).
Any new behavior associated with the opening of the four-pion cut is inherited directly from
E_{1}(2;t)
in the integrand.

There is a singularity at
\beta=1
, so there is no longer convenient access along the real line to any well-behaved limit.
Instead, we piggy-back off the spacelike solution by routing the numerical integrals along complex contours leading to an arbitrary point
\beta_{\star}>1
, from which they can continue to
\infty
along the real line.
Thus, for some arbitrary contour
\mathcal{C}
that goes from
\beta
to
\beta_{\star}
without crossing the cut,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]

\displaystyle=G_{2}\bigg[\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(1)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(1)}(\beta_{\star})+\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(1)}(\beta_{\star})+\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)-\int_{\mathcal{C}}\bigg(\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)g_{1}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}+\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\xi)\bigg)\mathrm{d}\xi\bigg]

\displaystyle-G_{1}\bigg[\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(2)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(2)}(\beta_{\star})+\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(2)}(\beta_{\star})-\int_{\mathcal{C}}\bigg(\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)g_{1}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}+h_{2}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)\bigg)\mathrm{d}\xi\bigg],

(80)

which is valid for all
t\in\mathbb{C}
except for the cut along
[4,\infty)
.

4.4
Practical considerations

There are numerous pitfalls in the method described above, so here we provide further details that are more closely aligned with how
\bar{E}_{5}
and
\bar{E}_{6}
are implemented in practice.

\bullet
Nothing about
section

4.3
is specific to
\bar{E}_{5}
, so the same integrals can be used for
\bar{E}_{6}
, further aided by the linearity of
\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]
in
G_{1},G_{2}
.
Schematically,

\bar{E}_{n}(4;t)=B_{n}+\mathcal{I}\big[\beta;G_{1n},G_{2n}\big]\,,

(81)

where

\begin{gathered}G_{15}=g_{1}(\beta)\,,\qquad G_{25}=g_{2}(\beta)\,,\qquad B_{5}=\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}E_{1}(2;\beta)\,,\\
G_{16}=-\frac{4}{t^{2}}-\frac{g_{1}(\beta)}{t}=0\,,\qquad G_{26}=-\frac{J_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to4.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-2.45348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

(82)

and
B_{6}
is the combination of
J_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to4.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-2.45348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.9 0 L 6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{(n)}
and (derivatives of)
E_{1}
appearing in
section

4
, minus that in
section

4
, minus
B_{5}/t
.

\bullet

Section

4.3
has the added benefit that
\beta_{\star}
can be held constant, meaning that many of the enclosed integrals need to be evaluated only once.
Partly for aesthetic reasons, we choose
\beta_{\star}=\sqrt{3}
, corresponding to
t_{\star}=\langle\beta\mapsto t\rangle(\sqrt{3})=-2
and
\tau_{\star}=\langle t\mapsto\tau\rangle(-2)=i/\sqrt{12}
, making it a rarity for which all of
\tau
,
t
and
\beta
are algebraic.
19
19

19

Other such points include
(t=-8,\tau=i/\sqrt{6},\beta=\sqrt{3/2})
and
(t=-32,\tau=i/\sqrt{3},\beta=3/\sqrt{8})
.

Evaluating the integrals using much higher working precision than what is otherwise needed, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(1)}(\beta_{\star})

\displaystyle=-0.897\,428\,059\,322\,249\,442\,551\,141\ldots\,,

(83)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(2)}(\beta_{\star})

\displaystyle=+0.007\,806\,884\,759\,652\,421\,539\,440\ldots\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(1)}(\beta_{\star})

\displaystyle=-6.114\,617\,584\,976\,858\,497\,987\,754\ldots\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(2)}(\beta_{\star})

\displaystyle=+2.827\,177\,949\,642\,984\,743\,645\,142\ldots\,.

These values are accurate to many more digits than shown here.

\bullet
A problem with
\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(n)}
and
\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(n)}
[recall
eqs.

71
and
76
] is that, while the integrals are theoretically convergent, numerical noise in the large-
\xi
limit of the integrands typically spoils that convergence in practice.
To remedy this, we perform the rearrangement (written here for a generic integrand
f
)

\int_{\beta}^{\infty}f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\beta}^{\chi}f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{\chi}^{\infty}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{\xi^{n}}\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\beta}^{\chi}f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{(1-n)\chi^{n-1}}\,,

(84)

where
f(\xi)=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{\xi^{n}}
when expanded around
\xi=\infty
.
We choose the cutoff
\chi
small enough that
\int_{\beta}^{\chi}f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi
can be evaluated numerically without noise issues, but large enough that the sum can be truncated, without loss of precision, at some finite
n=N
such that
\{a_{n}\}_{n=2}^{N}
are known.
We estimate the truncation error based on the relative difference between the exact and series-expanded integral at the cutoff, resulting in the uncertainty

\left|\frac{f(\chi)-\sum_{n=2}^{N}\frac{a_{n}}{\chi^{n}}}{f(\chi)}\right|\sum_{n=2}^{N}\frac{a_{n}}{(1-n)\chi^{n-1}}\,,

(85)

which we add in quadrature to the numerical uncertainty of
\int_{\beta}^{\chi}f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi
.
The optimal choice of
\chi
depends on the working precision of the numerics and the available
N
. In our case, we have found
\chi=10
to be a good choice for
\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(n)}
, and
\chi=100
for
\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(n)}
.
The Taylor expansions are given in
appendix

A
.

A related issue is that the numerical integration method we use (
quad
from the
mpmath
library
41
) does not account for the uncertainty of the integrand.
In lieu of proper error propagation, we may estimate the integrand-derived error of
\int_{\beta}^{\chi}f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi
as
\int_{\beta}^{\chi}\delta f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi
, where
\delta f
is the standard error of
f
.
With our choice of
\chi
, this contribution to the error is mostly negligible, so we normally omit it to speed up the computations.
This also holds for the numerically evaluated contour integrals.

\bullet
For
\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(n)}
, which is the numerically most expensive part of the calculation, a significant bottleneck is the conversion
\langle\beta\mapsto\tau\rangle(\xi)
, which is necessary for evaluating
E_{1}(2;\xi)
and which requires the complex machinery of
section

3.4
.
A more efficient approach is to perform the integral in the
\tau
-plane, where
E_{1}
can be evaluated directly: the function
\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle
needed to obtain
h_{n}(\xi)
is significantly cheaper than
\langle\beta\mapsto\tau\rangle
, as is the Jacobian

\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle^{\prime}(\tau)=-\frac{\pi i}{\beta t}\big[1+6\E(0,2,\psi_{t};q)\big]=\frac{\pi i}{\beta t}\frac{\big[\eta(q)\eta(q^{2})\big]^{4}+9\big[\eta(q^{3})\eta(q^{6})\big]^{4}}{\eta(q)\eta(q^{2})\eta(q^{3})\eta(q^{6})}\,,

(86)

where
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
and
t=\langle\tau\mapsto t\rangle(\tau)=\frac{4}{1-\beta^{2}}
, and the two variants follow from
eq.

32
and (
35
), respectively.

For contour integrals, we replace
\int_{\mathcal{C}}h_{n}(\beta)E_{1}(2;\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi
by

\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\tau}(\beta)}h_{n}\big(\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle(\tau)\big)E_{1}(2;\tau)\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle^{\prime}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\,,

(87)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\tau}(\beta)
is the straight-line contour from
\langle\beta\mapsto\tau\rangle(\beta)
to
\tau_{\star}=i/\sqrt{12}
.
Note that a nice property of this particular
\tau_{\star}
is that it is connected to any
\tau\in\langle t\mapsto\tau\rangle(\mathbb{C})
by a straight line entirely contained in
\langle t\mapsto\tau\rangle(\mathbb{C})
.
20
20

20

The image
\langle t\mapsto\tau\rangle(\mathbb{H})
, where
\mathbb{H}
is the upper half-plane, is the shaded portion of
fig.

2
(plus the extension to
\Im\tau=\infty
).
Its boundary is
\langle\theta\mapsto\tau\rangle(\mathbb{R})
.
Likewise,
\langle t\mapsto q\rangle(\mathbb{H})
is the shaded portion of
fig.

3
(shown in its entirety) and is bounded by
\langle\theta\mapsto q\rangle(\mathbb{R})
.
The images of the lower half-plane are obtained through
\tau\to-\tau^{*}
or
q\to q^{*}
.

Figure 8
:

Example of the process of computing
\bar{E}_{5}
and
\bar{E}_{6}
at
t_{\text{ex}}=4.2+i\epsilon
following
section

4.4
, with the final summation following
eq.

81
.
The integration contours are shown in the
\tau
and
\xi
planes, which are represented as in
fig.

2
.
Filled triangles indicate a complex integration contour, and empty ones a real integral toward infinity (using
\theta
for that along the imaginary
\tau
axis).
The shaded area indicates the “too close to the cut” zone described in the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\xi}(\beta)
.
For integrals using
eq.

84
, the parts from below (
<
) and above (
>
) the cutoff
\chi
are indicated.
All results are obtained with much higher precision than shown here.

For integrals along the real line, such as
\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(n)}(\beta_{\star})
, we run into the problem that
\langle t\mapsto\tau\rangle
, as given in
section

3.4
, breaks down for real
t
.
Instead, we turn to the
\theta
-parametrization of
eq.

58
, which allows
\int_{\beta}^{\infty}h_{2}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi
to be replaced by

\int_{\langle\beta\mapsto\theta\rangle(\beta)}^{\infty}h_{n}\big(\langle\theta\mapsto\beta\rangle(\theta)\big)E_{1}\big(2;\langle\theta\mapsto\tau\rangle(\theta)\big)\langle\theta\mapsto\beta\rangle^{\prime}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

(88)

and similarly for
\int_{\beta}^{\infty}\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi
.
While
\langle\theta\mapsto\beta\rangle
and
\langle\theta\mapsto\beta\rangle^{\prime}
are straightforward combinations of known functions, this is not the case for the inverse map
\langle\beta\mapsto\theta\rangle
.
However, it only needs to be applied twice—once to the integration limit, and once to
\chi
when using
eq.

84
—so a crude method is acceptable here.
Extrapolating
\langle\beta\mapsto\tau\rangle(\beta+i\epsilon)
to
\epsilon=0
and then inverting
eq.

58
is an option, but we use the simpler procedure of applying the Newton-Raphson method to the initial estimate
21
21

21

This is informed by asymptotic approximations of
\langle\theta\mapsto t\rangle(\theta)
for
\theta\to\pm\infty
, with some highly heuristic interpolation thereof to make the small-
t
region work.

\langle\theta\mapsto t\rangle^{\prime}(\theta)
goes as
e^{-1/|\theta|}
near
\theta\sim t\sim 0
, which poses a problem for convergence. Still, thanks to the availability of series expansions, there is no need to compute values very close to zero in this way.

\theta_{\text{est.}}=\begin{cases}-\tfrac{8}{\pi}\log(-t)&\text{if }t<-10\,,\\
\min(t,-3)&\text{if }-10\leq t<0\,,\\
\max(t,+3)&\text{if }0\leq t<20\,,\\
-\tfrac{48}{\pi\sqrt{3}}\log(+t)&\text{if }20\leq t\,,\end{cases}

(89)

which rapidly converges to a
\theta
such that
\langle\theta\mapsto t\rangle(\theta)=t
.

\bullet
For integrals not involving
E_{1}
, we keep the integration contour in the
\xi
-plane.
Sufficiently far below threshold, a straight line from
\beta
to
\beta_{\star}=\sqrt{3}
works. However, when
\Im\beta
is small, the line approaches the branch cut on
[0,1]
too closely, and we must reroute it.
Arbitrarily and conservatively defining “too close to the cut” as “intersecting the region where
|\Re(\beta)|<1
and
|\Im(\beta)|+\frac{|\Re(\beta)|}{|\Re(\beta_{\star})|}<1
”, we route the contour via the region’s corner point, as illustrated in
fig.

8
.
We denote this piecewise straight contour by
\mathcal{C}_{\xi}(\beta)
.

\bullet
With the contours in hand, a more detailed version of
section

4.3
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]

\displaystyle=G_{2}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(1)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(1)}(\beta_{\star})+\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(1)}(\beta_{\star})+\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{div}}(\beta)\Big]

\displaystyle-G_{1}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rat}}^{(2)}(\beta)+\mathcal{I}_{J}^{(2)}(\beta_{\star})+\mathcal{I}_{E}^{(2)}(\beta_{\star})\Big]

\displaystyle-G_{2}\bigg[\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\xi}(\beta)}\Big(\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)g_{1}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}-\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{div}}(\xi)\Big)\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\tau}(\beta)}h_{1}\big(\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle(\tau)\big)E_{1}(2;\tau)\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle^{\prime}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg]

\displaystyle+G_{1}\bigg[\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\xi}(\beta)}\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)g_{2}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\tau}(\beta)}h_{2}\big(\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle(\tau)\big)E_{1}(2;\tau)\langle\tau\mapsto\beta\rangle^{\prime}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg]\,.

(90)

Note that we have split
\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{reg}}
into its constituents according to
eq.

74
, since convergence is no issue here.
An illustrated overview of this equation at a specific
\beta
is given in
fig.

8
.

5
Results

Having derived means for computing the master integrals in the preceding sections, we are now ready to show the results and analyse the precision and performance of the various methods.
To recapitulate, the full set of methods is the following:

•

Bessel integrals
[
eqs.

11
,
12
, and
13
] allow for the computation of
E_{1,2,3}(2;t)
for
t\in(-\infty,16)
.
Via
eq.

10
, this also gives
\bar{E}_{4}(4;t)
.

•

Elliptic polylogarithms
[
section

3.1
]
allow the same integrals to be computed for all
t
, granted that that
t
can be translated to
q
via the methods of
section

3.4
.

•

Eisenstein series
[
section

3.2
]
is a variant of the elliptic polylogarithm method, aiming for greater numerical efficiency.

•

Poisson resummation
[
section

3.3
]
is another variant, based on the Eisenstein series but aiming for greater small-
t
stability.

•

Wrońskian on the real line
[
sections

4.1
and
4.2
]
allows for the computation of
\bar{E}_{5,6}(4;t)
for
t\in(-\infty,16)
, based on some underlying implementation of
E_{1}(2;t)
.
In practice, we only use this method for the precomputation at
t=t_{\star}=-2
, i.e.,
eq.

83
.

•

Contour-integrated Wrońskian
[
sections

4.3
and
4.4
]
extends the previous method to all
t
and is numerically faster, given that the integrals at
t_{\star}
have been precomputed.

•

Series expansion
(
appendix

A
) is the most dependable method for
t
close to zero.
Its radius of convergence is
16
for
E_{1,2,3}(2;t)
and
4
for
\bar{E}_{4,5,6}(4;t)
.
It is trivial to obtain very high-order series expansions from the differential equations, and for high-precision applications, we have settled on a 32nd-order one.

•

pySecDec

9
;
10
;
25
;
26
is used as our main benchmark.
It allows for the computation of all master integrals at all
t
, although for
\Re(t)
above threshold it is typically only dependable for
\Im(t)\geq 0
.

•

AMFlow

34
is another benchmark that works for all master integrals and all real
t
.
It is very precise but also very slow and, as we will see below, not always accurate.

•

FeynTrop

8
;
7
is another, faster benchmark, but is restricted to the finite integrals
E_{1,2,3}(2;t)
[although
\bar{E}_{4}(2;t)
is accessible through
eq.

10
].

Note that our methods are not meant to compete with the general-purpose codes
pySecDec
,
AMFlow
, and
FeynTrop
. Rather, we have implemented them because they deliver more precision, are faster or more stable for the particular integrals considered in this paper.

5.1
The master integrals as functions of
t
